    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what made me finally come in is how I feel after I have these eating episodes. It’s not just that I ate too much or whatever. It’s like afterward I feel really gross, and just so disappointed in myself, like I should have been able to stop or have more control.\n\nAnd then I kind of sit with it and make it worse in my head. I’ll write in my journal afterward, and it turns into me writing these really awful things about myself. Like I’m angry at myself, or disgusted with myself, and I know that sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels really true. I just don’t want to keep feeling like that after it happens.\n\nDoctor: After these eating episodes, do you ever try to compensate for what you’ve eaten, like making yourself vomit, using laxatives, or doing extra exercise?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? I mean, not in the way you’re describing. I don’t make myself throw up or anything like that, and I’m not, like, taking stuff after to try to undo it.\n\nThe exercise thing is a little harder for me to answer because sometimes I’ll think, like, “Okay, tomorrow I need to be better,” or “I should move more,” but I don’t really have some planned punishment workout or anything. It’s more the thoughts afterward, feeling awful about it, not so much doing something specific to make up for it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen, and do they usually happen in response to a specific feeling, like stress or sadness?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty regular, honestly. For the past several months, it’s usually at least once a week—most often at night after work—where I’ll stop and buy too much food, more than I really need, and then I end up eating all of it in one stretch.\n\nI keep trying to tell myself it was just a bad week or that I’ll get back on track, but when I think about it, I can’t really remember a recent month where it stopped for more than maybe a week or so. It’s not every single day, but it’s consistent enough that I notice it, and I kind of expect it now, which feels awful to say.\n\nAs for the feeling part… I’m not totally sure how to separate it out, but it does seem to happen after work a lot, especially when I’m worn down or in my head. Like the night just kind of turns into that before I’ve really talked myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: When you're having one of these eating episodes, do you feel like you're not in control of how much you're eating or that you can't stop once you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, that’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly. It doesn’t feel like a normal decision once it’s started. At first I’ll tell myself I’m just going to have some of it, or just eat enough to feel full, but then it’s like I keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically.\n\nAnd the weird thing is I’m not completely unaware, you know? I’m thinking while it’s happening. I’ll literally have this voice in my head like, “Fumi, stop, you have sketches to finish,” or “You don’t even want this anymore,” but my body just keeps going. I’ll open the next container or unwrap the next thing, and part of me is watching myself do it and feeling upset, but I still don’t stop.\n\nUsually it only ends when the food is gone, or when I feel so uncomfortable that continuing would just make me feel sick. So… yeah, it really does feel like I can’t control how much I’m eating once I’ve crossed that line.\n\nDoctor: Outside of these eating episodes, do you find yourself feeling down, anxious, or really hard on yourself most days, even when there’s no food involved?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I’m definitely hard on myself a lot, but it’s hard for me to tell where that ends and where this whole eating thing starts, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I wouldn’t say I wake up every single day feeling sad for no reason, exactly. Some days I’m just tired or annoyed or stressed in a normal way, and I can go to work and do what I need to do. But I do catch myself kind of criticizing everything I do—how I look, whether I’m being lazy, whether I’m falling behind—stuff like that. It’s not always loud, but it’s there.\n\nAnxious… maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure I’d call it that most days. I don’t want to exaggerate it. It’s more like this low-level pressure in my head, and then after the eating episodes it gets way worse. But outside of those, I guess I’m not totally sure how to separate what’s normal stress from something more than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling particularly upset or preoccupied about your body shape or weight, especially in relation to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I do think about my body, and I don’t always feel great about it, especially after one of those nights, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s the main thing driving it.\n\nAfter I eat like that, I’ll feel really gross and embarrassed, and then I’ll start looking at myself differently, like my clothes feel wrong or I feel heavier even if that probably doesn’t make sense. But before it happens, I’m not always sitting there thinking, “I hate my body,” or anything that clear. Sometimes it’s more like I just don’t want to think about myself at all.\n\nWeight is… I don’t know. It’s in the background. I notice it, I worry a little, but I’m not weighing myself constantly or planning everything around it. It’s more that the eating episode makes me feel upset with myself, and then my body becomes part of what I’m upset about. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong preoccupation or distress about your body shape or weight most of the time, not just after eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s most of the time, no… or at least, not in a really intense way.\n\nLike, I’m aware of my body. I have moments where I don’t like how I look, or I’ll feel uncomfortable in certain clothes, or I’ll catch myself comparing myself to someone, especially if I’m already feeling bad. But it’s not like I spend the whole day obsessing over my weight or checking myself constantly. I don’t think my entire mood is built around the number or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more noticeable after I’ve eaten too much, because then everything feels worse and I get this kind of disgusted feeling with myself. But on normal days, I can usually go to work, do my cleaning route, think about other things, talk to people, and not be stuck on my body the whole time.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say it bothers me sometimes, but I wouldn’t call it a strong preoccupation most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Outside of these eating episodes and the feelings that come right after them, do you find that your daily functioning — like work, relationships, or other responsibilities — is significantly affected by your thoughts or mood?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not in a way where I’m missing work all the time or completely falling apart, if that’s what you mean. I still get up and go to my cleaning shifts. I still do what I’m supposed to do, and if there’s a design event or something I said I’d go to, I usually still make myself show up.\n\nBut it does affect me, just maybe more quietly. The day after one of those episodes, I’ll be at work pushing the cart around or cleaning bathrooms or whatever, and I’m doing the tasks, but my head is somewhere else. I’ll keep replaying it and feeling embarrassed, like I’m carrying this gross secret around. And then I feel ashamed for being distracted, because it’s not like anyone else knows why I’m acting weird or slower.\n\nWith the design stuff, it’s kind of specific. I sketch and keep notes, and sometimes after an episode I’ll write these really harsh journal entries, just dumping everything I’m feeling. Then later I don’t want to even open the journal because I know those pages are in there. So I’ll put off sketching, even if I had an idea, because looking at the notebook feels like having to look at myself from that night again.\n\nSo… I’m functioning, technically. I’m showing up. But the next day especially, I’m distracted and ashamed, and it can make me avoid things I actually care about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel marked distress about your body shape or weight, even when you’re not having an eating episode?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it marked distress, no. I mean, I do have feelings about my body, and there are days where I’ll put something on and feel uncomfortable or kind of disappointed, and then I’ll change clothes three times and get annoyed with myself. But if you mean like, am I constantly upset about my shape or weight even when nothing happened with eating… I don’t think so.\n\nIt’s more like it gets louder after I’ve overeaten. Then I feel really ashamed and my body becomes this thing I focus on, like everything feels wrong. But on a regular day, I can usually move through things without being stuck on it. I might notice my reflection or compare myself for a second, but then I’m back to work or errands or whatever I’m doing.\n\nSo… I guess it bothers me sometimes, but not in that big, constant way. It’s not absent, but it’s not the main thing in my head most of the time.\n\nDoctor: During these eating episodes, would you say you feel a complete loss of control, like you can’t stop eating even if you want to, or is it more that you feel like you shouldn’t be eating that much but still feel in control while you’re doing it?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I lose control, honestly. I don’t know if I’d say I’m completely blank or anything, because I am aware of what I’m doing, but once I start, it’s like I keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically.\n\nI’ll even be thinking in my head, like, “Fumi, stop. You have sketches to finish. You don’t need this. Just put it away.” And I’ll mean it when I think it, but then my hand is still opening something else, or I’m still going back for more. It’s like the part of me that knows I should stop is talking, but it’s not actually in charge.\n\nUsually I don’t really stop because I made a calm decision to stop. I stop because the food is gone, or because I feel so uncomfortable that I can’t keep pretending it’s fine. That’s the part that scares me afterward, because during it I keep thinking I’m about to stop, like after this one thing, after this last bite, and then I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes usually happen after you've been fasting or following a strict diet or eating plan?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean… I don’t think of myself as someone who fasts or follows some strict plan, not in an organized way. I’m not, like, counting every calorie or doing a named diet or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll tell myself I’m going to “eat better” the next day, or I’ll skip something because I feel bad about what happened before, but I don’t know if that counts as fasting. It’s not usually this clear thing where I’ve been on a strict eating plan and then that directly leads to an episode. At least, I haven’t noticed that pattern.\n\nIt feels more messy than that. Like I might have a day where my meals are weird because of work or because I’m distracted, but I wouldn’t say, “Oh, every time I restrict, then it happens.” I’m not sure I could track it that neatly. So… no, not usually after a strict diet, I don’t think. Or if it is connected sometimes, I haven’t really paid attention enough to say that confidently.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific time when one of these eating episodes happened, and you hadn’t skipped meals or been restricting your food beforehand?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I can think of times like that. Not always with, like, an exact date, but a pretty common version is after work. I’ll come home from cleaning, and I’ll already have eaten something normal after my shift, so it’s not like I’ve been starving myself all day or skipped everything.\n\nLike, I’ll have dinner or at least enough food that I know I’m not actually hungry, and then I’ll sit down because I’m tired, or I’ll try to work on a design idea or sketch for something, and if I get stuck, I start feeling restless. Then I’ll go into the kitchen and open something—chips, crackers, whatever’s easy—and at first I tell myself it’s just a little snack while I think.\n\nBut a lot of the time I’m not even hungry. That’s the weird part. I can feel that I’m not hungry, like my body isn’t asking for food, but I’ll still keep opening things and eating them. It feels more like I’m tired or frustrated or just stuck in my head, and the eating starts happening anyway. So yeah, there have definitely been episodes where I hadn’t restricted beforehand. I had eaten, and then it still happened.\n\nDoctor: On average, how many times a week do these eating episodes happen, and have you noticed them happening consistently over a period of at least three months?\n\nPatient: I think… on average it’s at least once a week. Sometimes it might be more than that, depending on the week, but the pattern I can say for sure is there’s usually one night where it happens.\n\nA lot of times it’s after work. I’ll be on my way home and stop somewhere, and I’ll buy way more food than I meant to—like more than makes sense for one person for one night—and then when I get home I end up eating all of it in one stretch. Not like a normal meal, more like I just keep going until it’s gone.\n\nAnd yeah, it’s been going on for several months. I can’t really think of a recent month where it completely stopped. Maybe there are weeks where it doesn’t happen, or it’s not as bad, but I don’t think it’s gone away for more than a week or so at a time. It feels pretty steady, which is embarrassing to say, but… yeah, at least weekly for the past few months.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your feelings about your body shape or weight are a major reason you feel so upset after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do feel bad about my body sometimes, and after it happens I can definitely have thoughts like, “Why did I do that?” or I’ll feel gross or uncomfortable in my clothes or just kind of ashamed. But I don’t know if I’d say my body shape or weight is the main reason I’m upset afterward.\n\nIt’s more like… the upset feeling is all tangled together. Part of it is about how my body feels, like being too full or feeling like I messed up. Part of it is the loss of control, like I told myself I wasn’t going to do it and then I did anyway. And then I get mad at myself because it feels wasteful or embarrassing, especially if I bought all this food and ate it alone.\n\nSo I guess body and weight thoughts are in there sometimes, but I don’t know if they’re the major reason. I haven’t really separated it out like that before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I don’t know if I’d call it “marked,” I guess. Like, I definitely have days where I don’t like how I look, or I’ll notice my clothes fitting a certain way and get kind of self-conscious, but I don’t think I’m sitting around all day constantly distressed about my weight or shape when nothing has happened.

For the past several months, it’s usually at least once a week—most often at night after work—where I’ll stop and buy too much food, more than I really need, and then I end up eating all of it in one stretch.\n\nI keep trying to tell myself it was just a bad week or that I’ll get back on track, but when I think about it, I can’t really remember a recent month where it stopped for more than maybe a week or so. Like the night just kind of turns into that before I’ve really talked myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: When you're having one of these eating episodes, do you feel like you're not in control of how much you're eating or that you can't stop once you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, that’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly. It doesn’t feel like a normal decision once it’s started. At first I’ll tell myself I’m just going to have some of it, or just eat enough to feel full, but then it’s like I keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically.\n\nAnd the weird thing is I’m not completely unaware, you know? I’m thinking while it’s happening. I’ll literally have this voice in my head like, “Fumi, stop, you have sketches to finish,” or “You don’t even want this anymore,” but my body just keeps going. I’ll open the next container or unwrap the next thing, and part of me is watching myself do it and feeling upset, but I still don’t stop.\n\nUsually it only ends when the food is gone, or when I feel so uncomfortable that continuing would just make me feel sick. So… yeah, it really does feel like I can’t control how much I’m eating once I’ve crossed that line.\n\nDoctor: Outside of these eating episodes, do you find yourself feeling down, anxious, or really hard on yourself most days, even when there’s no food involved?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I’m definitely hard on myself a lot, but it’s hard for me to tell where that ends and where this whole eating thing starts, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I wouldn’t say I wake up every single day feeling sad for no reason, exactly. Some days I’m just tired or annoyed or stressed in a normal way, and I can go to work and do what I need to do. But I do catch myself kind of criticizing everything I do—how I look, whether I’m being lazy, whether I’m falling behind—stuff like that. It’s not always loud, but it’s there.\n\nAnxious… maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure I’d call it that most days. I don’t want to exaggerate it. It’s more like this low-level pressure in my head, and then after the eating episodes it gets way worse. I mean… I do think about my body, and I don’t always feel great about it, especially after one of those nights, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s the main thing driving it.\n\nAfter I eat like that, I’ll feel really gross and embarrassed, and then I’ll start looking at myself differently, like my clothes feel wrong or I feel heavier even if that probably doesn’t make sense. But before it happens, I’m not always sitting there thinking, “I hate my body,” or anything that clear. Sometimes it’s more like I just don’t want to think about myself at all.\n\nWeight is… I don’t know. It’s in the background. I notice it, I worry a little, but I’m not weighing myself constantly or planning everything around it. It’s more that the eating episode makes me feel upset with myself, and then my body becomes part of what I’m upset about. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong preoccupation or distress about your body shape or weight most of the time, not just after eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s most of the time, no… or at least, not in a really intense way.\n\nLike, I’m aware of my body. I have moments where I don’t like how I look, or I’ll feel uncomfortable in certain clothes, or I’ll catch myself comparing myself to someone, especially if I’m already feeling bad. But it’s not like I spend the whole day obsessing over my weight or checking myself constantly. I don’t think my entire mood is built around the number or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more noticeable after I’ve eaten too much, because then everything feels worse and I get this kind of disgusted feeling with myself. But on normal days, I can usually go to work, do my cleaning route, think about other things, talk to people, and not be stuck on my body the whole time.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say it bothers me sometimes, but I wouldn’t call it a strong preoccupation most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Outside of these eating episodes and the feelings that come right after them, do you find that your daily functioning — like work, relationships, or other responsibilities — is significantly affected by your thoughts or mood?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not in a way where I’m missing work all the time or completely falling apart, if that’s what you mean. I still get up and go to my cleaning shifts. I still do what I’m supposed to do, and if there’s a design event or something I said I’d go to, I usually still make myself show up.\n\nBut it does affect me, just maybe more quietly. The day after one of those episodes, I’ll be at work pushing the cart around or cleaning bathrooms or whatever, and I’m doing the tasks, but my head is somewhere else. I’ll keep replaying it and feeling embarrassed, like I’m carrying this gross secret around. And then I feel ashamed for being distracted, because it’s not like anyone else knows why I’m acting weird or slower.\n\nWith the design stuff, it’s kind of specific. I sketch and keep notes, and sometimes after an episode I’ll write these really harsh journal entries, just dumping everything I’m feeling. Then later I don’t want to even open the journal because I know those pages are in there. So I’ll put off sketching, even if I had an idea, because looking at the notebook feels like having to look at myself from that night again.\n\nSo… I’m functioning, technically. I’m showing up. But the next day especially, I’m distracted and ashamed, and it can make me avoid things I actually care about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel marked distress about your body shape or weight, even when you’re not having an eating episode?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it marked distress, no. I mean, I do have feelings about my body, and there are days where I’ll put something on and feel uncomfortable or kind of disappointed, and then I’ll change clothes three times and get annoyed with myself. But if you mean like, am I constantly upset about my shape or weight even when nothing happened with eating… I don’t think so.\n\nIt’s more like it gets louder after I’ve overeaten. Then I feel really ashamed and my body becomes this thing I focus on, like everything feels wrong. But on a regular day, I can usually move through things without being stuck on it. I might notice my reflection or compare myself for a second, but then I’m back to work or errands or whatever I’m doing.\n\nSo… I guess it bothers me sometimes, but not in that big, constant way. It’s not absent, but it’s not the main thing in my head most of the time.\n\nDoctor: During these eating episodes, would you say you feel a complete loss of control, like you can’t stop eating even if you want to, or is it more that you feel like you shouldn’t be eating that much but still feel in control while you’re doing it?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I lose control, honestly. I don’t know if I’d say I’m completely blank or anything, because I am aware of what I’m doing, but once I start, it’s like I keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically.\n\nI’ll even be thinking in my head, like, “Fumi, stop. You have sketches to finish. You don’t need this. Just put it away.” And I’ll mean it when I think it, but then my hand is still opening something else, or I’m still going back for more. It’s like the part of me that knows I should stop is talking, but it’s not actually in charge.\n\nUsually I don’t really stop because I made a calm decision to stop. I stop because the food is gone, or because I feel so uncomfortable that I can’t keep pretending it’s fine. That’s the part that scares me afterward, because during it I keep thinking I’m about to stop, like after this one thing, after this last bite, and then I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes usually happen after you've been fasting or following a strict diet or eating plan?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean… I don’t think of myself as someone who fasts or follows some strict plan, not in an organized way. I’m not, like, counting every calorie or doing a named diet or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll tell myself I’m going to “eat better” the next day, or I’ll skip something because I feel bad about what happened before, but I don’t know if that counts as fasting. It’s not usually this clear thing where I’ve been on a strict eating plan and then that directly leads to an episode. At least, I haven’t noticed that pattern.\n\nIt feels more messy than that. Like I might have a day where my meals are weird because of work or because I’m distracted, but I wouldn’t say, “Oh, every time I restrict, then it happens.” I’m not sure I could track it that neatly. So… no, not usually after a strict diet, I don’t think. Or if it is connected sometimes, I haven’t really paid attention enough to say that confidently.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific time when one of these eating episodes happened, and you hadn’t skipped meals or been restricting your food beforehand?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I can think of times like that. Not always with, like, an exact date, but a pretty common version is after work. I’ll come home from cleaning, and I’ll already have eaten something normal after my shift, so it’s not like I’ve been starving myself all day or skipped everything.\n\nLike, I’ll have dinner or at least enough food that I know I’m not actually hungry, and then I’ll sit down because I’m tired, or I’ll try to work on a design idea or sketch for something, and if I get stuck, I start feeling restless. Then I’ll go into the kitchen and open something—chips, crackers, whatever’s easy—and at first I tell myself it’s just a little snack while I think.\n\nBut a lot of the time I’m not even hungry. That’s the weird part. I can feel that I’m not hungry, like my body isn’t asking for food, but I’ll still keep opening things and eating them. It feels more like I’m tired or frustrated or just stuck in my head, and the eating starts happening anyway. So yeah, there have definitely been episodes where I hadn’t restricted beforehand. I had eaten, and then it still happened.\n\nDoctor: On average, how many times a week do these eating episodes happen, and have you noticed them happening consistently over a period of at least three months?\n\nPatient: I think… on average it’s at least once a week. Sometimes it might be more than that, depending on the week, but the pattern I can say for sure is there’s usually one night where it happens.\n\nA lot of times it’s after work. I’ll be on my way home and stop somewhere, and I’ll buy way more food than I meant to—like more than makes sense for one person for one night—and then when I get home I end up eating all of it in one stretch. Not like a normal meal, more like I just keep going until it’s gone.\n\nAnd yeah, it’s been going on for several months. I can’t really think of a recent month where it completely stopped. Maybe there are weeks where it doesn’t happen, or it’s not as bad, but I don’t think it’s gone away for more than a week or so at a time. It feels pretty steady, which is embarrassing to say, but… yeah, at least weekly for the past few months.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your feelings about your body shape or weight are a major reason you feel so upset after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do feel bad about my body sometimes, and after it happens I can definitely have thoughts like, “Why did I do that?” or I’ll feel gross or uncomfortable in my clothes or just kind of ashamed. But I don’t know if I’d say my body shape or weight is the main reason I’m upset afterward.\n\nIt’s more like… the upset feeling is all tangled together. Part of it is about how my body feels, like being too full or feeling like I messed up. Part of it is the loss of control, like I told myself I wasn’t going to do it and then I did anyway. And then I get mad at myself because it feels wasteful or embarrassing, especially if I bought all this food and ate it alone.\n\nSo I guess body and weight thoughts are in there sometimes, but I don’t know if they’re the major reason. I haven’t really separated it out like that before.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the shame and upset you feel after eating episodes, do you experience marked distress about your body shape or weight on a regular basis, even when you haven't had an episode?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “marked,” I guess. Like, I definitely have days where I don’t like how I look, or I’ll notice my clothes fitting a certain way and get kind of self-conscious, but I don’t think I’m sitting around all day constantly distressed about my weight or shape when nothing has happened.\n\nIt’s more noticeable after an episode, because then I feel really aware of my body and uncomfortable and embarrassed. On regular days, it comes and goes. I might avoid looking too long in the mirror, or I might compare myself to somebody for a second, but then I’m usually distracted by work or errands or whatever I’m doing.\n\nSo… I guess outside of those times, it’s there sometimes, but I’m not sure it’s this major ongoing distress. I haven’t really tracked it that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

Sometimes it might be more than that, depending on the week, but the pattern I can say for sure is there’s usually one night where it happens.\n\nA lot of times it’s after work. I’ll be on my way home and stop somewhere, and I’ll buy way more food than I meant to—like more than makes sense for one person for one night—and then when I get home I end up eating all of it in one stretch. Not like a normal meal, more like I just keep going until it’s gone.\n\nAnd yeah, it’s been going on for several months. I can’t really think of a recent month where it completely stopped. Maybe there are weeks where it doesn’t happen, or it’s not as bad, but I don’t think it’s gone away for more than a week or so at a time. It feels pretty steady, which is embarrassing to say, but… yeah, at least weekly for the past few months.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your feelings about your body shape or weight are a major reason you feel so upset after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do feel bad about my body sometimes, and after it happens I can definitely have thoughts like, “Why did I do that?” or I’ll feel gross or uncomfortable in my clothes or just kind of ashamed. But I don’t know if I’d say my body shape or weight is the main reason I’m upset afterward.\n\nIt’s more like… the upset feeling is all tangled together. I haven’t really separated it out like that before.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the shame and upset you feel after eating episodes, do you experience marked distress about your body shape or weight on a regular basis, even when you haven't had an episode?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “marked,” I guess. I haven’t really tracked it that way.\n\n[Last Question]\nOutside of eating episodes and their immediate aftermath, do you find yourself feeling intense distress or preoccupation about your body shape or weight on a regular basis?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 24 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so, not in that intense way. I mean, I’m aware of my body, and I can be self-conscious, especially if my clothes feel tight or I catch myself in a mirror at a bad angle or something. But outside of those eating nights and right after, I don’t think I’m regularly consumed by it.

It’s more like background noise sometimes, if that makes sense. Like I might think, “I don’t like how I look today,” or I might compare myself to someone for a minute, but then I’m usually just going to work or cleaning or dealing with whatever else is happening. It doesn’t usually take over the whole day.

After an episode it’s different, because then I feel much more upset and embarrassed and aware of my body. But on normal days, I wouldn’t say it’s an intense preoccupation. At least I haven’t noticed it being like that.

But before it happens, I’m not always sitting there thinking, “I hate my body,” or anything that clear. Sometimes it’s more like I just don’t want to think about myself at all.\n\nWeight is… I don’t know. It’s in the background. I notice it, I worry a little, but I’m not weighing myself constantly or planning everything around it. It’s more that the eating episode makes me feel upset with myself, and then my body becomes part of what I’m upset about. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong preoccupation or distress about your body shape or weight most of the time, not just after eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s most of the time, no… or at least, not in a really intense way.\n\nLike, I’m aware of my body. I have moments where I don’t like how I look, or I’ll feel uncomfortable in certain clothes, or I’ll catch myself comparing myself to someone, especially if I’m already feeling bad. I still get up and go to my cleaning shifts. I still do what I’m supposed to do, and if there’s a design event or something I said I’d go to, I usually still make myself show up.\n\nBut it does affect me, just maybe more quietly. The day after one of those episodes, I’ll be at work pushing the cart around or cleaning bathrooms or whatever, and I’m doing the tasks, but my head is somewhere else. I’ll keep replaying it and feeling embarrassed, like I’m carrying this gross secret around. I don’t know if I’d say I’m completely blank or anything, because I am aware of what I’m doing, but once I start, it’s like I keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically.\n\nI’ll even be thinking in my head, like, “Fumi, stop. That’s the weird part. I can feel that I’m not hungry, like my body isn’t asking for food, but I’ll still keep opening things and eating them. It feels more like I’m tired or frustrated or just stuck in my head, and the eating starts happening anyway. So yeah, there have definitely been episodes where I hadn’t restricted beforehand. I had eaten, and then it still happened.\n\nDoctor: On average, how many times a week do these eating episodes happen, and have you noticed them happening consistently over a period of at least three months?\n\nPatient: I think… on average it’s at least once a week. I notice it, I worry a little, but I’m not weighing myself constantly or planning everything around it. It’s more that the eating episode makes me feel upset with myself, and then my body becomes part of what I’m upset about. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong preoccupation or distress about your body shape or weight most of the time, not just after eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s most of the time, no… or at least, not in a really intense way.\n\nLike, I’m aware of my body. I have moments where I don’t like how I look, or I’ll feel uncomfortable in certain clothes, or I’ll catch myself comparing myself to someone, especially if I’m already feeling bad. But it’s not like I spend the whole day obsessing over my weight or checking myself constantly. I don’t think my entire mood is built around the number or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more noticeable after I’ve eaten too much, because then everything feels worse and I get this kind of disgusted feeling with myself. But on normal days, I can usually go to work, do my cleaning route, think about other things, talk to people, and not be stuck on my body the whole time.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say it bothers me sometimes, but I wouldn’t call it a strong preoccupation most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Outside of these eating episodes and the feelings that come right after them, do you find that your daily functioning — like work, relationships, or other responsibilities — is significantly affected by your thoughts or mood?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not in a way where I’m missing work all the time or completely falling apart, if that’s what you mean. I still get up and go to my cleaning shifts. I still do what I’m supposed to do, and if there’s a design event or something I said I’d go to, I usually still make myself show up.\n\nBut it does affect me, just maybe more quietly. The day after one of those episodes, I’ll be at work pushing the cart around or cleaning bathrooms or whatever, and I’m doing the tasks, but my head is somewhere else. I’ll keep replaying it and feeling embarrassed, like I’m carrying this gross secret around. And then I feel ashamed for being distracted, because it’s not like anyone else knows why I’m acting weird or slower.\n\nWith the design stuff, it’s kind of specific. I sketch and keep notes, and sometimes after an episode I’ll write these really harsh journal entries, just dumping everything I’m feeling. Then later I don’t want to even open the journal because I know those pages are in there. So I’ll put off sketching, even if I had an idea, because looking at the notebook feels like having to look at myself from that night again.\n\nSo… I’m functioning, technically. I’m showing up. But the next day especially, I’m distracted and ashamed, and it can make me avoid things I actually care about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel marked distress about your body shape or weight, even when you’re not having an eating episode?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it marked distress, no. I mean, I do have feelings about my body, and there are days where I’ll put something on and feel uncomfortable or kind of disappointed, and then I’ll change clothes three times and get annoyed with myself. But if you mean like, am I constantly upset about my shape or weight even when nothing happened with eating… I don’t think so.\n\nIt’s more like it gets louder after I’ve overeaten. Then I feel really ashamed and my body becomes this thing I focus on, like everything feels wrong. But on a regular day, I can usually move through things without being stuck on it. I might notice my reflection or compare myself for a second, but then I’m back to work or errands or whatever I’m doing.\n\nSo… I guess it bothers me sometimes, but not in that big, constant way. It’s not absent, but it’s not the main thing in my head most of the time.\n\nDoctor: During these eating episodes, would you say you feel a complete loss of control, like you can’t stop eating even if you want to, or is it more that you feel like you shouldn’t be eating that much but still feel in control while you’re doing it?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I lose control, honestly. I don’t know if I’d say I’m completely blank or anything, because I am aware of what I’m doing, but once I start, it’s like I keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically.\n\nI’ll even be thinking in my head, like, “Fumi, stop. You have sketches to finish. You don’t need this. Just put it away.” And I’ll mean it when I think it, but then my hand is still opening something else, or I’m still going back for more. It’s like the part of me that knows I should stop is talking, but it’s not actually in charge.\n\nUsually I don’t really stop because I made a calm decision to stop. I stop because the food is gone, or because I feel so uncomfortable that I can’t keep pretending it’s fine. That’s the part that scares me afterward, because during it I keep thinking I’m about to stop, like after this one thing, after this last bite, and then I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes usually happen after you've been fasting or following a strict diet or eating plan?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean… I don’t think of myself as someone who fasts or follows some strict plan, not in an organized way. I’m not, like, counting every calorie or doing a named diet or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll tell myself I’m going to “eat better” the next day, or I’ll skip something because I feel bad about what happened before, but I don’t know if that counts as fasting. It’s not usually this clear thing where I’ve been on a strict eating plan and then that directly leads to an episode. At least, I haven’t noticed that pattern.\n\nIt feels more messy than that. Like I might have a day where my meals are weird because of work or because I’m distracted, but I wouldn’t say, “Oh, every time I restrict, then it happens.” I’m not sure I could track it that neatly. So… no, not usually after a strict diet, I don’t think. Or if it is connected sometimes, I haven’t really paid attention enough to say that confidently.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific time when one of these eating episodes happened, and you hadn’t skipped meals or been restricting your food beforehand?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I can think of times like that. Not always with, like, an exact date, but a pretty common version is after work. I’ll come home from cleaning, and I’ll already have eaten something normal after my shift, so it’s not like I’ve been starving myself all day or skipped everything.\n\nLike, I’ll have dinner or at least enough food that I know I’m not actually hungry, and then I’ll sit down because I’m tired, or I’ll try to work on a design idea or sketch for something, and if I get stuck, I start feeling restless. Then I’ll go into the kitchen and open something—chips, crackers, whatever’s easy—and at first I tell myself it’s just a little snack while I think.\n\nBut a lot of the time I’m not even hungry. That’s the weird part. I can feel that I’m not hungry, like my body isn’t asking for food, but I’ll still keep opening things and eating them. It feels more like I’m tired or frustrated or just stuck in my head, and the eating starts happening anyway. So yeah, there have definitely been episodes where I hadn’t restricted beforehand. I had eaten, and then it still happened.\n\nDoctor: On average, how many times a week do these eating episodes happen, and have you noticed them happening consistently over a period of at least three months?\n\nPatient: I think… on average it’s at least once a week. Sometimes it might be more than that, depending on the week, but the pattern I can say for sure is there’s usually one night where it happens.\n\nA lot of times it’s after work. I’ll be on my way home and stop somewhere, and I’ll buy way more food than I meant to—like more than makes sense for one person for one night—and then when I get home I end up eating all of it in one stretch. Not like a normal meal, more like I just keep going until it’s gone.\n\nAnd yeah, it’s been going on for several months. I can’t really think of a recent month where it completely stopped. Maybe there are weeks where it doesn’t happen, or it’s not as bad, but I don’t think it’s gone away for more than a week or so at a time. It feels pretty steady, which is embarrassing to say, but… yeah, at least weekly for the past few months.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your feelings about your body shape or weight are a major reason you feel so upset after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do feel bad about my body sometimes, and after it happens I can definitely have thoughts like, “Why did I do that?” or I’ll feel gross or uncomfortable in my clothes or just kind of ashamed. But I don’t know if I’d say my body shape or weight is the main reason I’m upset afterward.\n\nIt’s more like… the upset feeling is all tangled together. Part of it is about how my body feels, like being too full or feeling like I messed up. Part of it is the loss of control, like I told myself I wasn’t going to do it and then I did anyway. And then I get mad at myself because it feels wasteful or embarrassing, especially if I bought all this food and ate it alone.\n\nSo I guess body and weight thoughts are in there sometimes, but I don’t know if they’re the major reason. I haven’t really separated it out like that before.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the shame and upset you feel after eating episodes, do you experience marked distress about your body shape or weight on a regular basis, even when you haven't had an episode?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “marked,” I guess. Like, I definitely have days where I don’t like how I look, or I’ll notice my clothes fitting a certain way and get kind of self-conscious, but I don’t think I’m sitting around all day constantly distressed about my weight or shape when nothing has happened.\n\nIt’s more noticeable after an episode, because then I feel really aware of my body and uncomfortable and embarrassed. On regular days, it comes and goes. I might avoid looking too long in the mirror, or I might compare myself to somebody for a second, but then I’m usually distracted by work or errands or whatever I’m doing.\n\nSo… I guess outside of those times, it’s there sometimes, but I’m not sure it’s this major ongoing distress. I haven’t really tracked it that way.\n\nDoctor: Outside of eating episodes and their immediate aftermath, do you find yourself feeling intense distress or preoccupation about your body shape or weight on a regular basis?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that intense way. I mean, I’m aware of my body, and I can be self-conscious, especially if my clothes feel tight or I catch myself in a mirror at a bad angle or something. But outside of those eating nights and right after, I don’t think I’m regularly consumed by it.\n\nIt’s more like background noise sometimes, if that makes sense. Like I might think, “I don’t like how I look today,” or I might compare myself to someone for a minute, but then I’m usually just going to work or cleaning or dealing with whatever else is happening. It doesn’t usually take over the whole day.\n\nAfter an episode it’s different, because then I feel much more upset and embarrassed and aware of my body. But on normal days, I wouldn’t say it’s an intense preoccupation. At least I haven’t noticed it being like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it’s more like I can’t stop, even though technically I know I’m the one doing it, if that makes sense.

Like once I start, it feels like my hand just keeps going to the next thing almost automatically. I’ll be thinking, “Fumi, stop, you have sketches to finish,” or “You said you were only going to have a little bit,” and I really do mean it in my head. But then I don’t actually stop. I keep opening something else, or going back for more, and it feels like there’s this momentum to it.

It’s not like I’m calmly deciding, “Okay, I choose to keep eating and I’m fine with it.” I usually already know I shouldn’t keep going, and I’m kind of arguing with myself while it’s happening. But the stopping part doesn’t happen until the food is gone, or until I feel so uncomfortable that I basically have to stop. So… I guess it feels less like a choice and more like I lose control once I’ve started.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what made me finally come in is how I feel after I have these eating episodes. It’s not just that I ate too much or whatever. It’s like afterward I feel really gross, and just so disappointed in myself, like I should have been able to stop or have more control.\n\nAnd then I kind of sit with it and make it worse in my head. I’ll write in my journal afterward, and it turns into me writing these really awful things about myself. Like I’m angry at myself, or disgusted with myself, and I know that sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels really true. I just don’t want to keep feeling like that after it happens.\n\nDoctor: After these eating episodes, do you ever try to compensate for what you’ve eaten, like making yourself vomit, using laxatives, or doing extra exercise?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? I mean, not in the way you’re describing. I don’t make myself throw up or anything like that, and I’m not, like, taking stuff after to try to undo it.\n\nThe exercise thing is a little harder for me to answer because sometimes I’ll think, like, “Okay, tomorrow I need to be better,” or “I should move more,” but I don’t really have some planned punishment workout or anything. It’s more the thoughts afterward, feeling awful about it, not so much doing something specific to make up for it.\n\nDoctor: How often do these eating episodes happen, and do they usually happen in response to a specific feeling, like stress or sadness?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty regular, honestly. For the past several months, it’s usually at least once a week—most often at night after work—where I’ll stop and buy too much food, more than I really need, and then I end up eating all of it in one stretch.\n\nI keep trying to tell myself it was just a bad week or that I’ll get back on track, but when I think about it, I can’t really remember a recent month where it stopped for more than maybe a week or so. It’s not every single day, but it’s consistent enough that I notice it, and I kind of expect it now, which feels awful to say.\n\nAs for the feeling part… I’m not totally sure how to separate it out, but it does seem to happen after work a lot, especially when I’m worn down or in my head. Like the night just kind of turns into that before I’ve really talked myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: When you're having one of these eating episodes, do you feel like you're not in control of how much you're eating or that you can't stop once you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, that’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly. It doesn’t feel like a normal decision once it’s started. At first I’ll tell myself I’m just going to have some of it, or just eat enough to feel full, but then it’s like I keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically.\n\nAnd the weird thing is I’m not completely unaware, you know? I’m thinking while it’s happening. I’ll literally have this voice in my head like, “Fumi, stop, you have sketches to finish,” or “You don’t even want this anymore,” but my body just keeps going. I’ll open the next container or unwrap the next thing, and part of me is watching myself do it and feeling upset, but I still don’t stop.\n\nUsually it only ends when the food is gone, or when I feel so uncomfortable that continuing would just make me feel sick. So… yeah, it really does feel like I can’t control how much I’m eating once I’ve crossed that line.\n\nDoctor: Outside of these eating episodes, do you find yourself feeling down, anxious, or really hard on yourself most days, even when there’s no food involved?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I’m definitely hard on myself a lot, but it’s hard for me to tell where that ends and where this whole eating thing starts, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, I wouldn’t say I wake up every single day feeling sad for no reason, exactly. Some days I’m just tired or annoyed or stressed in a normal way, and I can go to work and do what I need to do. But I do catch myself kind of criticizing everything I do—how I look, whether I’m being lazy, whether I’m falling behind—stuff like that. It’s not always loud, but it’s there.\n\nAnxious… maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure I’d call it that most days. I don’t want to exaggerate it. It’s more like this low-level pressure in my head, and then after the eating episodes it gets way worse. But outside of those, I guess I’m not totally sure how to separate what’s normal stress from something more than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling particularly upset or preoccupied about your body shape or weight, especially in relation to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I do think about my body, and I don’t always feel great about it, especially after one of those nights, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s the main thing driving it.\n\nAfter I eat like that, I’ll feel really gross and embarrassed, and then I’ll start looking at myself differently, like my clothes feel wrong or I feel heavier even if that probably doesn’t make sense. But before it happens, I’m not always sitting there thinking, “I hate my body,” or anything that clear. Sometimes it’s more like I just don’t want to think about myself at all.\n\nWeight is… I don’t know. It’s in the background. I notice it, I worry a little, but I’m not weighing myself constantly or planning everything around it. It’s more that the eating episode makes me feel upset with myself, and then my body becomes part of what I’m upset about. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong preoccupation or distress about your body shape or weight most of the time, not just after eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s most of the time, no… or at least, not in a really intense way.\n\nLike, I’m aware of my body. I have moments where I don’t like how I look, or I’ll feel uncomfortable in certain clothes, or I’ll catch myself comparing myself to someone, especially if I’m already feeling bad. But it’s not like I spend the whole day obsessing over my weight or checking myself constantly. I don’t think my entire mood is built around the number or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more noticeable after I’ve eaten too much, because then everything feels worse and I get this kind of disgusted feeling with myself. But on normal days, I can usually go to work, do my cleaning route, think about other things, talk to people, and not be stuck on my body the whole time.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say it bothers me sometimes, but I wouldn’t call it a strong preoccupation most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Outside of these eating episodes and the feelings that come right after them, do you find that your daily functioning — like work, relationships, or other responsibilities — is significantly affected by your thoughts or mood?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not in a way where I’m missing work all the time or completely falling apart, if that’s what you mean. I still get up and go to my cleaning shifts. I still do what I’m supposed to do, and if there’s a design event or something I said I’d go to, I usually still make myself show up.\n\nBut it does affect me, just maybe more quietly. The day after one of those episodes, I’ll be at work pushing the cart around or cleaning bathrooms or whatever, and I’m doing the tasks, but my head is somewhere else. I’ll keep replaying it and feeling embarrassed, like I’m carrying this gross secret around. And then I feel ashamed for being distracted, because it’s not like anyone else knows why I’m acting weird or slower.\n\nWith the design stuff, it’s kind of specific. I sketch and keep notes, and sometimes after an episode I’ll write these really harsh journal entries, just dumping everything I’m feeling. Then later I don’t want to even open the journal because I know those pages are in there. So I’ll put off sketching, even if I had an idea, because looking at the notebook feels like having to look at myself from that night again.\n\nSo… I’m functioning, technically. I’m showing up. But the next day especially, I’m distracted and ashamed, and it can make me avoid things I actually care about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel marked distress about your body shape or weight, even when you’re not having an eating episode?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it marked distress, no. I mean, I do have feelings about my body, and there are days where I’ll put something on and feel uncomfortable or kind of disappointed, and then I’ll change clothes three times and get annoyed with myself. But if you mean like, am I constantly upset about my shape or weight even when nothing happened with eating… I don’t think so.\n\nIt’s more like it gets louder after I’ve overeaten. Then I feel really ashamed and my body becomes this thing I focus on, like everything feels wrong. But on a regular day, I can usually move through things without being stuck on it. I might notice my reflection or compare myself for a second, but then I’m back to work or errands or whatever I’m doing.\n\nSo… I guess it bothers me sometimes, but not in that big, constant way. It’s not absent, but it’s not the main thing in my head most of the time.\n\nDoctor: During these eating episodes, would you say you feel a complete loss of control, like you can’t stop eating even if you want to, or is it more that you feel like you shouldn’t be eating that much but still feel in control while you’re doing it?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I lose control, honestly. I don’t know if I’d say I’m completely blank or anything, because I am aware of what I’m doing, but once I start, it’s like I keep reaching for the next thing almost automatically.\n\nI’ll even be thinking in my head, like, “Fumi, stop. You have sketches to finish. You don’t need this. Just put it away.” And I’ll mean it when I think it, but then my hand is still opening something else, or I’m still going back for more. It’s like the part of me that knows I should stop is talking, but it’s not actually in charge.\n\nUsually I don’t really stop because I made a calm decision to stop. I stop because the food is gone, or because I feel so uncomfortable that I can’t keep pretending it’s fine. That’s the part that scares me afterward, because during it I keep thinking I’m about to stop, like after this one thing, after this last bite, and then I just… don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do these eating episodes usually happen after you've been fasting or following a strict diet or eating plan?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean… I don’t think of myself as someone who fasts or follows some strict plan, not in an organized way. I’m not, like, counting every calorie or doing a named diet or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll tell myself I’m going to “eat better” the next day, or I’ll skip something because I feel bad about what happened before, but I don’t know if that counts as fasting. It’s not usually this clear thing where I’ve been on a strict eating plan and then that directly leads to an episode. At least, I haven’t noticed that pattern.\n\nIt feels more messy than that. Like I might have a day where my meals are weird because of work or because I’m distracted, but I wouldn’t say, “Oh, every time I restrict, then it happens.” I’m not sure I could track it that neatly. So… no, not usually after a strict diet, I don’t think. Or if it is connected sometimes, I haven’t really paid attention enough to say that confidently.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific time when one of these eating episodes happened, and you hadn’t skipped meals or been restricting your food beforehand?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I can think of times like that. Not always with, like, an exact date, but a pretty common version is after work. I’ll come home from cleaning, and I’ll already have eaten something normal after my shift, so it’s not like I’ve been starving myself all day or skipped everything.\n\nLike, I’ll have dinner or at least enough food that I know I’m not actually hungry, and then I’ll sit down because I’m tired, or I’ll try to work on a design idea or sketch for something, and if I get stuck, I start feeling restless. Then I’ll go into the kitchen and open something—chips, crackers, whatever’s easy—and at first I tell myself it’s just a little snack while I think.\n\nBut a lot of the time I’m not even hungry. That’s the weird part. I can feel that I’m not hungry, like my body isn’t asking for food, but I’ll still keep opening things and eating them. It feels more like I’m tired or frustrated or just stuck in my head, and the eating starts happening anyway. So yeah, there have definitely been episodes where I hadn’t restricted beforehand. I had eaten, and then it still happened.\n\nDoctor: On average, how many times a week do these eating episodes happen, and have you noticed them happening consistently over a period of at least three months?\n\nPatient: I think… on average it’s at least once a week. Sometimes it might be more than that, depending on the week, but the pattern I can say for sure is there’s usually one night where it happens.\n\nA lot of times it’s after work. I’ll be on my way home and stop somewhere, and I’ll buy way more food than I meant to—like more than makes sense for one person for one night—and then when I get home I end up eating all of it in one stretch. Not like a normal meal, more like I just keep going until it’s gone.\n\nAnd yeah, it’s been going on for several months. I can’t really think of a recent month where it completely stopped. Maybe there are weeks where it doesn’t happen, or it’s not as bad, but I don’t think it’s gone away for more than a week or so at a time. It feels pretty steady, which is embarrassing to say, but… yeah, at least weekly for the past few months.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your feelings about your body shape or weight are a major reason you feel so upset after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do feel bad about my body sometimes, and after it happens I can definitely have thoughts like, “Why did I do that?” or I’ll feel gross or uncomfortable in my clothes or just kind of ashamed. But I don’t know if I’d say my body shape or weight is the main reason I’m upset afterward.\n\nIt’s more like… the upset feeling is all tangled together. Part of it is about how my body feels, like being too full or feeling like I messed up. Part of it is the loss of control, like I told myself I wasn’t going to do it and then I did anyway. And then I get mad at myself because it feels wasteful or embarrassing, especially if I bought all this food and ate it alone.\n\nSo I guess body and weight thoughts are in there sometimes, but I don’t know if they’re the major reason. I haven’t really separated it out like that before.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the shame and upset you feel after eating episodes, do you experience marked distress about your body shape or weight on a regular basis, even when you haven't had an episode?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “marked,” I guess. Like, I definitely have days where I don’t like how I look, or I’ll notice my clothes fitting a certain way and get kind of self-conscious, but I don’t think I’m sitting around all day constantly distressed about my weight or shape when nothing has happened.\n\nIt’s more noticeable after an episode, because then I feel really aware of my body and uncomfortable and embarrassed. On regular days, it comes and goes. I might avoid looking too long in the mirror, or I might compare myself to somebody for a second, but then I’m usually distracted by work or errands or whatever I’m doing.\n\nSo… I guess outside of those times, it’s there sometimes, but I’m not sure it’s this major ongoing distress. I haven’t really tracked it that way.\n\nDoctor: Outside of eating episodes and their immediate aftermath, do you find yourself feeling intense distress or preoccupation about your body shape or weight on a regular basis?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that intense way. I mean, I’m aware of my body, and I can be self-conscious, especially if my clothes feel tight or I catch myself in a mirror at a bad angle or something. But outside of those eating nights and right after, I don’t think I’m regularly consumed by it.\n\nIt’s more like background noise sometimes, if that makes sense. Like I might think, “I don’t like how I look today,” or I might compare myself to someone for a minute, but then I’m usually just going to work or cleaning or dealing with whatever else is happening. It doesn’t usually take over the whole day.\n\nAfter an episode it’s different, because then I feel much more upset and embarrassed and aware of my body. But on normal days, I wouldn’t say it’s an intense preoccupation. At least I haven’t noticed it being like that.\n\nDoctor: During these eating episodes, would you say you feel like you can't stop eating even when you want to, or do you feel like you're choosing to keep eating even if you know you shouldn't?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s more like I can’t stop, even though technically I know I’m the one doing it, if that makes sense.\n\nLike once I start, it feels like my hand just keeps going to the next thing almost automatically. I’ll be thinking, “Fumi, stop, you have sketches to finish,” or “You said you were only going to have a little bit,” and I really do mean it in my head. But then I don’t actually stop. I keep opening something else, or going back for more, and it feels like there’s this momentum to it.\n\nIt’s not like I’m calmly deciding, “Okay, I choose to keep eating and I’m fine with it.” I usually already know I shouldn’t keep going, and I’m kind of arguing with myself while it’s happening. But the stopping part doesn’t happen until the food is gone, or until I feel so uncomfortable that I basically have to stop. So… I guess it feels less like a choice and more like I lose control once I’ve started.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’ll come home from cleaning, and I’ll already have eaten something normal after my shift, so it’s not like I’ve been starving myself all day or skipped everything.\n\nLike, I’ll have dinner or at least enough food that I know I’m not actually hungry, and then I’ll sit down because I’m tired, or I’ll try to work on a design idea or sketch for something, and if I get stuck, I start feeling restless. Then I’ll go into the kitchen and open something—chips, crackers, whatever’s easy—and at first I tell myself it’s just a little snack while I think.\n\nBut a lot of the time I’m not even hungry. That’s the weird part. I can feel that I’m not hungry, like my body isn’t asking for food, but I’ll still keep opening things and eating them. It feels more like I’m tired or frustrated or just stuck in my head, and the eating starts happening anyway. So yeah, there have definitely been episodes where I hadn’t restricted beforehand. I had eaten, and then it still happened.\n\nDoctor: On average, how many times a week do these eating episodes happen, and have you noticed them happening consistently over a period of at least three months?\n\nPatient: I think… on average it’s at least once a week. Sometimes it might be more than that, depending on the week, but the pattern I can say for sure is there’s usually one night where it happens.\n\nA lot of times it’s after work. I’ll be on my way home and stop somewhere, and I’ll buy way more food than I meant to—like more than makes sense for one person for one night—and then when I get home I end up eating all of it in one stretch. Not like a normal meal, more like I just keep going until it’s gone.\n\nAnd yeah, it’s been going on for several months. I can’t really think of a recent month where it completely stopped. Maybe there are weeks where it doesn’t happen, or it’s not as bad, but I don’t think it’s gone away for more than a week or so at a time. It feels pretty steady, which is embarrassing to say, but… yeah, at least weekly for the past few months.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your feelings about your body shape or weight are a major reason you feel so upset after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do feel bad about my body sometimes, and after it happens I can definitely have thoughts like, “Why did I do that?” or I’ll feel gross or uncomfortable in my clothes or just kind of ashamed. But I don’t know if I’d say my body shape or weight is the main reason I’m upset afterward.\n\nIt’s more like… the upset feeling is all tangled together. Part of it is about how my body feels, like being too full or feeling like I messed up. Part of it is the loss of control, like I told myself I wasn’t going to do it and then I did anyway. And then I get mad at myself because it feels wasteful or embarrassing, especially if I bought all this food and ate it alone.\n\nSo I guess body and weight thoughts are in there sometimes, but I don’t know if they’re the major reason. I haven’t really separated it out like that before.\n\nDoctor: Outside of the shame and upset you feel after eating episodes, do you experience marked distress about your body shape or weight on a regular basis, even when you haven't had an episode?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “marked,” I guess. Like, I definitely have days where I don’t like how I look, or I’ll notice my clothes fitting a certain way and get kind of self-conscious, but I don’t think I’m sitting around all day constantly distressed about my weight or shape when nothing has happened.\n\nIt’s more noticeable after an episode, because then I feel really aware of my body and uncomfortable and embarrassed. On regular days, it comes and goes. I might avoid looking too long in the mirror, or I might compare myself to somebody for a second, but then I’m usually distracted by work or errands or whatever I’m doing.\n\nSo… I guess outside of those times, it’s there sometimes, but I’m not sure it’s this major ongoing distress. I haven’t really tracked it that way.\n\nDoctor: Outside of eating episodes and their immediate aftermath, do you find yourself feeling intense distress or preoccupation about your body shape or weight on a regular basis?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that intense way. I mean, I’m aware of my body, and I can be self-conscious, especially if my clothes feel tight or I catch myself in a mirror at a bad angle or something. But outside of those eating nights and right after, I don’t think I’m regularly consumed by it.\n\nIt’s more like background noise sometimes, if that makes sense. Like I might think, “I don’t like how I look today,” or I might compare myself to someone for a minute, but then I’m usually just going to work or cleaning or dealing with whatever else is happening. It doesn’t usually take over the whole day.\n\nAfter an episode it’s different, because then I feel much more upset and embarrassed and aware of my body. But on normal days, I wouldn’t say it’s an intense preoccupation. At least I haven’t noticed it being like that.\n\nDoctor: During these eating episodes, would you say you feel like you can't stop eating even when you want to, or do you feel like you're choosing to keep eating even if you know you shouldn't?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s more like I can’t stop, even though technically I know I’m the one doing it, if that makes sense.\n\nLike once I start, it feels like my hand just keeps going to the next thing almost automatically. I’ll be thinking, “Fumi, stop, you have sketches to finish,” or “You said you were only going to have a little bit,” and I really do mean it in my head. But then I don’t actually stop. I keep opening something else, or going back for more, and it feels like there’s this momentum to it.\n\nIt’s not like I’m calmly deciding, “Okay, I choose to keep eating and I’m fine with it.” I usually already know I shouldn’t keep going, and I’m kind of arguing with myself while it’s happening. But the stopping part doesn’t happen until the food is gone, or until I feel so uncomfortable that I basically have to stop. So… I guess it feels less like a choice and more like I lose control once I’ve started."
